zone411 [user?id=zone411] 15 hours ago [item?id=50637782] | next [#50637909] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

For context, OpenAI's 4 new papers resolve 8 problems of the Top 500 Open Problems in Math list (LLM ranked, https://proofatlas.ai/open-problems/ [https://proofatlas.ai/open-problems/]).
They include:
#25 — Matrix-multiplication exponent equals two. The paper gives the exact exponent 9/4 over every field, refuting the conjecture.
#89 — Strongly polynomial linear programming (Smale’s ninth problem). The same paper also gives polynomial-time algorithms for
#64 — Mean-payoff games,
#65 — Parity games,
#340 — Condon’s simple stochastic games.
#183 — Polynomial diameter bound for polytopes,
#130 — Polynomial pivot rule for the simplex method, both refuted. Some pairs of vertices are superpolynomially far apart.
#137 — Erdős–Rado sunflower conjecture. For every fixed number of petals r, the paper proves the conjectured Cᵣᵏ bound.
(rankings post Riemann (#2) and VP vs VNP (#31)).

_alternator_ [user?id=_alternator_] 13 hours ago [item?id=50638676] | parent [#50637782] | next [#50638233] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I've just started looking at where these results are coming from, and it seems that these four results actually all stem from a more fundamental result. This is in contrast to many of the other papers where disparate results come from more ad hoc and disjointed arguments.
"The other papers in this collection use different aspects of this framework. Shared labels occur in the sunflower argument [29], and high-dimensional placements distinguish cuts of arithmetic computations [26]. Spectral comparison and network compression enter the circuit lower bounds [28]. Further applications concern Fourier transforms, integer multiplication on tapes, and matrix multiplication [25, 27, 30]."
That's a fundamental theoretical breakthrough, and they've done some work to present it more cleanly than the other results. It's an interesting result on it's own, but the link between this theoretical result and the other, more impactful results will take a while for me to understand. TBH this is the first of these mathpocalypse papers where I really want to understand the proofs because it feels like there is something truly worth knowing here.
https://github.com/openai/math/blob/main/preprints/Euclidean... [https://github.com/openai/math/blob/main/preprints/Euclidean-geometry-in-the-asymptotic-spectrum-of-tensors-November-24-2026/paper.pdf]

phkahler [user?id=phkahler] 3 hours ago [item?id=50642774] | root [#50637782] | parent [#50641458] | next [#50642307] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

From that article: "Unlike a human, Astra was able to tackle the problem from both ends at once: working forward from the scant facts of the 1808 journey pictured in the sketchbook and backwards from the immense number of surviving facts about 1808 British North America in general."
The only things superhuman in that story are the AIs ability to run down every lead and maintain a huge context to make connections. That is a real superpower that it has, but the author even says the only way a human could have found the result is if they made it their lifes work. AI isn't super human, it's super fast, super persistent, and has a large working memory.
It also helps that due to training on the entire internet, it comes with a general (and often detailed) knowledge of every topic known to man. That's its starting point. When I have AI do work on open source software, there is an advantage that commercial software does not get. All of the big AIs have already seen our source code so the high level structure is part of it training, which means that level does not need to be learned in-context.

Is say that there is some truth to the yes side.
Humans typically narrow their focus of expertise the deeper they go into a topic. Medicine is a good example. Ask two very different kinds of doctors to diagnose a patient and they are highly likely to lean towards their specialist knowledge and wisdom and experience.
To use an old analogy when all you have is a hammer, everything looks like a nail.
It think LLM’s have the ability to make large scale left field jumps because they are a mixture of expert knowledge. Something that has been solved in one field can be applied in others where it wouldn’t be obvious to someone that only has depth in one of those fields.

I don't know if this statement is intended to be self evident, I can see it reading both for and against those skill sets leading to unique insights.
My suspicion has always been that Physicists are rare, Physicists who are also also experts in Architecture (or any other deep, specialist discipline) are much rarer still, and that a person who inhabits a low cardinality region of a many-dimensional knowledge Venn diagram can probably provide many unique and valuable insights.
LLMs are this in the most extreme case, we simply need to coordinate them to explore all of the combinatorial spaces.

I think this is that sort of thing, mostly from mathematics, but not entirely.
Imagine a person who could read every book and recall relevant parts that pertained to a certain problem. They could use all that to solve this sort of problem, drawing from all relevant facts and ideas.
But this isn't (super) human intelligence, it's merely superhuman processing, much like computers doing billions of calculations per second is superhuman. It works for academic fields like mathematics because all the knowledge is published and is in textbooks.
This is very positive for mathematicians (and others) who can now develop ideas and intuitions then exhaustively search existing knowledge to develop solutions and proofs using LLMs. Obvious applications in Comp Sci as well.

Arkhaine_kupo [user?id=Arkhaine_kupo] 9 hours ago [item?id=50639816] | root [#50637782] | parent [#50639735] | next [#50639811] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

> it will enable new capabilities to knock out more things humans have not managed to up to this point.
This has not been proven btw. Experiments where training sets have had obvious cut offs, like before the discovery of general relativity or quantum theory where unable to make the leaps neccesary for new areas of discovery.
what we are seeing in math is that some problems have convergent solutions, therefore methodologically if you can define a loss function that you can minimise, they seem to be able to work through it until they reach a final answer.
But most advances in math are not methodological, they are categorical. Topology doesnt come from thinking about a better solution for matrix multiplication. It comes from thinking about deforming spaces.

Almost no one has made the claim that, in November 2026, we have AI systems that can do things like derive general relativity with a data cutoff before it.
"It hasn't been proven the models can do this" could've been said about every AI news story 6 months before the story. I think it is extremely likely that AIs will, in fact, knock out more things humans have not managed.
I didn't really get "qualitative AI skepticism" even in 2024,. One can debate if it'll solve the Riemann hypothesis by 2026 or by 2040, and if it'll cure most diseases by 2036 or 2066, but that's just haggling; surely the core question "will we have intelligent machines and are current systems near a permanent intelligence ceiling" has already been sorted.

pegasus [user?id=pegasus] 7 hours ago [item?id=50640653] | root [#50637782] | parent [#50640382] | next [#50640511] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I think they are just saying that no one was expecting AI to make such conceptual leaps this year, but surely they will get there soon enough. Which completely ignores the distinction between categorical (theory making) and methodological (problem solving) GP has drawn.
Personally, I doubt digital systems in general, not just transformers, will ever crack that frontier. For one, I think we underestimate the value of countless life experiences accreted in every cell of our bodies. We are basically of the same stuff as the universe, whereas the computational systems we build inhabit a secondary world of representations. Sure, we can give them sensors, but then we would also need to give them a few million or billion years of interacting with the physical world so they can distill that into something abstract enough to guide them in the ethereal realms of high maths. I find Henri Bergson's work very enlightening in this matter, especially the distinction he draws between time and duration.

 [vote?id=50640511&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220#50640511]
Arkhaine_kupo [user?id=Arkhaine_kupo] 8 hours ago [item?id=50640511] | root [#50637782] | parent [#50640183] | prev [#50640382] | next [#50641049] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

> in November 2026, we have AI systems that can do things like derive general relativity with a data cutoff before it.
The test proposed by DeepMind as a sign of AGI that no one has even gotten close to? That Sam Altman believes would be attemptable in 2028?
Please share a source, cause you will make a shit ton of money if OpenAI and Google haven't heard about it
> "It hasn't been proven the models can do this" could've been said about every AI news story 6 months before the story.
No. People who know little about math or computer science keep getting impressed. People who follow the space have all been quite aligned in expectations.
The biggest leaps where going from RL to Transformers, because the lineral relationship between compute/training and performance solved the issues of overfitting epoch and finicky training
and the agentic harnessing which allowed for creating minimising function end goals that then worked in essentially a model loop that works towards reaching the assigned goal.
With those two breakthroughs almost everyone could predict they would work well for some math problems that have convergent answers, that they would work well for computer science which has testable expectations and so on.
In the same way that we can predict that some of the inherent limitations of LLM vector spaces will probably make it a dead end for a fully embodied AGI, and why people like Yann Lecunn are more interested in world models. Why latent space modeling and things like the Plato Representation Hypothesis are gaining a lot of attention and so on.
> I didn't really get "qualitative AI skepticism" even in 2024,
Because there are plenty of fuzzy domains, there are non convergent solution problems, there are problems related to resource sharing and alignment (politics) which all quickly escape the abilities of agentic harneshing. You cannot solve the halting problem by throwing agents at it
> if it'll cure most diseases by 2036 or 2066,
Ai is obviously a huge space, but you cannot somehow pretend that for example any medical advance resulting from AlphaFold protein library counts as an AI solved disease.
Also this discussion is about LLMs for obvious reasons, so neural nets finding x-ray anomalies would not be part of the discussion either.
> surely the core question "will we have intelligent machines and are current systems near a permanent intelligence ceiling" has already been sorted.
It hasnt. Which is why the last few biggest releases of almost all big providers have been around cost being reduced massively and small incremental upgrades to frontier modelling.
The logarithmic curve of emergent abilities seems to be showing more clearly than a potential exponential AGI run off to superintelligence

We need to make 2 important notes here
1) the RH solution has not been verified and understood. Preliminary results show its not exploiting lean bugs, but the math community has not yet accepted, understood and verified the results
2) Terrence Tao is a genius but is also not going to be always right.
As a counterpoint to his lack of foresight, here is a user of HN asking about the lack of RH solutions when they gave their last Millenium prize update
https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49796590 [https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49796590]
the direction of the work was evident enough for users in this site, even if Tao was less optimistic

>The test proposed by DeepMind as a sign of AGI that no one has even gotten close to? That Sam Altman believes would be attemptable in 2028?
But end of 2028 is not 2026.
(He didn't make any specific prediction about when it may be able to do that, but he said he thinks it is a definition of AGI and that he thinks there will be AGI by end of 2028. To me, that would be closer to ASI and not AGI.)
>No. People who know little about math or computer science keep getting impressed. People who follow the space have all been quite aligned in expectations.
Every day I see recommended videos and tweets and blogs from esteemed math and computer science professors and researchers and they're all blown away. Even the ones who don't like how non-human-aimed the proofs are all say they are extremely impressed.
>In the same way that we can predict that some of the inherent limitations of LLM vector spaces will probably make it a dead end for a fully embodied AGI

> and why people like Yann Lecunn are more interested in world models. Why latent space modeling and things like the Plato Representation Hypothesis are gaining a lot of attention and so on.
LeCun is essentially a crank at this point. If there is ever an AGI-type system that beats LLMs by using a completely different architecture, it is very likely it will only exist as early as it does because it will be built by AGI LLMs. Scaling LLMs will be enough for AGI and maybe even ASI, albeit inefficiently.
I think you and I just need to wait until end of 2028 and see which of us was more correct.

I also think when minimised, if possible, most of the proofs wont include many novel approaches. They will rely on proofs by contradiction and other well trodden paths, many of which can probably be expressed much more neatly than 250k pages

bdauvergne [user?id=bdauvergne] 1 hour ago [item?id=50644163] | root [#50637782] | parent [#50640826] | next [#50643359] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Brute force with pruning of impossible/bad branches is still brute-force. LLM are good at pruning. Making thousand of mathematician look at the same problem at the same time is brute force too, with few force. Infinite foresight would be to immediately jump to conclusions, that would not be intelligence, it would be magic. LLM are not magical, will never be. Obligatory citation:
"Any sufficiently advanced technology is indistinguishable from magic." Arthur C. Clarke

ogogmad [user?id=ogogmad] 14 hours ago [item?id=50638085] | parent [#50637782] | prev [#50640303] | next [#50640997] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

3 out of the 9: We now know the exact difficulty of multiplying integers, polynomials and matrices. The first two conditionally (Weak Exponential Time Hypothesis), and the last one unconditionally.

... to be pedantic, we know the exact difficulty of matrix multiplication when their elements can be multiplied or added in constant time.

 [vote?id=50638734&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220#50638734]
Maxatar [user?id=Maxatar] 12 hours ago [item?id=50638734] | root [#50637782] | parent [#50638214] | next [#50640997] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

The bound is on the number of arithmetic operations, irrespective of how computationally expensive those operations are. Constant time arithmetic is only an assumption you need to make if you want to equate arithmetic complexity with time complexity which is not what this result is about.
It's similar to how various optimal sorting algorithms have a complexity of O(nlogn), but that is a bound on the number of comparison operations, irrespective of how computationally expensive any individual comparison operation is.

reply [reply?id=50638734&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220%2350638734]

 [vote?id=50641963&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220#50641963]
rerdavies [user?id=rerdavies] 4 hours ago [item?id=50641963] | root [#50637782] | parent [#50638734] | next [#50640997] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Tragically, the bound in the paper is for number of multiplies and divides (each counted as one operation). Additions, subtractions, and memory accesses are all free.
Allow me to float the hypothesis that the number of L1 cache misses in a Matrix multiply is O(n^9/4). Just in case there are any brilliant mathematicians looking for a new problem to solve.
Yours sincerely,
Somebody who quickly discovered that reducing the number of multiplies, while dramatically increasing the number of additions doesn't actually make your matrix multiply run faster.

We've seen so much interesting work come out of OpenAI and Anthropic in the last few weeks. It really feels like both have tapped into the pioneering spirit of the old Bell Labs.
But, Bell Labs existed as part of an agreement with the government that AT&T's monopoly would be used for the public good. OpenAI and Anthropic don't really have much use for this beyond PR...
If even the prestige from Riemann's Hypothesis couldn't boost ANT, what is the reason for them devoting such a huge fraction of their R&D budget to unrelated (though impactful) problems? Scaring mathematicians into burning their non-existent research budgets on tokens in order to make a couple million dollars?
Open weight models are to the point that most business cases are completely solved by them, at a fraction of the price of Claude, Copilot, or OpenAI. Unfortunately, the average business doesn't care that the AI can solve frontier problems: they just need something to summarize emails or give an executive a summary of the day's headlines. The market is just waiting for a cheap provider that is just as turnkey... Which isn't exactly a high barrier these days. IMO there is space for a unicorn to emerge in the next few years to eat everyone's lunch.

>Open weight models are to the point that most business cases are completely solved by them, at a fraction of the price of Claude
We just bought our second "AI server" (a box with ~256gb ram and a 96gb vram RTX6000) to run open weight models, because we're getting ~$2900/day of "sonnet quality" tokens out of it and allows us to do silly things like summarize project emails and do sentiment analysis when they're not running at 100% capacity. Plus the fact that after 4 months all the tokens are fully ROI'd and everything is just the cost of electricity. I don't know how Anthropic, OpenAI etc aren't going to race to the bottom with the Chinese trying to capture business bulk AI market. I guess Luna was an attempt at that, but as long as Anthropic keeps charging $$$ for Sonnet, it makes our ROI numbers look ridiculously good. Plus data sovereignty; none of our data leaves he building, and all the other side benefits.
The second box has room for 10x RTX6000, so we expect to keep buying more as we ramp up over the next year.

 [vote?id=50640580&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220#50640580]
hadlock [user?id=hadlock] 7 hours ago [item?id=50640580] | root [#50637909] | parent [#50640385] | next [#50640686] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

The model I'm using right now for batch processing is Qwen 3.5 35B-A3B unsloth at NVFP4 which is the native quant for the RTX6000 (perf. bonus) I get about 650-1080 tokens/s aggregate between concurrency 48-70 with an average of 160,000 context and I forget the exact K/V cache but i think it was Q8. Compared to sonnet 4.6 (which was their top sonnet model until recently) which was a real dog, but Anthropic's mid-tier model for a long time, the model specified above just totally rocks it's socks off. Sonnet 5.5 (which came out more recently) feels like a quantized version of Opus 5 and people seem to think it's actually worth the squeeze; Q3.5 35B-A3b does not compare favorably to Sonnet 5.5 in intelligence, but it's wicked fast for common business tasks like db/crm enrichment, which honestly you don't need a smart model for, you just need one with a low error rate. Which is what 35b-a3b is. For anyone just barely following along, the A3B is the magic number, it's a MoE model and only activates 3B which makes it ludicrously fast for doing things like summarizing a million documents (which is about 1TB in my case) in a month. But it also still has very good tool calling error rate (8% for qwen vs 4.5% for sonnet 4.6). Obviously Sonnet 5.5 is better but that's a problem for future me when/if our business-class tasks get that complex.

hadlock [user?id=hadlock] 7 hours ago [item?id=50640771] | root [#50637909] | parent [#50640686] | next [#50641610] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

>presumably because they get a rough approximation
Correct; however the main point I was trying to make was: supporting OP's point that most business processes simply do not need very smart AI to get done. They just don't. Most batch processing AI tasks don't need a 50b model, because they're by nature short horizon, modular, templated tasks. We were on Qwen 3.5 era models and when the Qwen 3.8 models came out (which included a 27B "dense" model, but no 35B-A3B MoE)... we saw no reason to "upgrade" to the "smarter" model, because we would take an enormous speed/performance hit.
We're upgrading to 256GB system ram because Qwen Flash Next is an A10B model which is quite "smart" and you can hold the inactive "experts" in system ram and swap them out with a 80% prediction rate. But we'll keep the other server running the 35B-A3B probably for a long time, it's an excellent, extremely fast batch processing model for what we're doing with it.

rybosome [user?id=rybosome] 3 hours ago [item?id=50642455] | root [#50637909] | parent [#50641888] | next [#50645234] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

This use case would require API key based usage rather than subscription based, and that’s an enormous cost difference.
I don’t know the exact numbers, but anecdotally: I was burning easily $200 per day in tokens from API keys for a research factory setup recently. To reduce cost I went through the trouble of inverting the cloud based execution model so it could run on my laptop and use a subscription from a coding agent by driving the coding agent directly. Now, at a $200/month sub, I am just tapping out my weekly usage by letting this thing run 24/7.
Loosely that’s a 30x cost difference, and that doesn’t even take into account parallelism. OP mentioned being able to get up to 100 concurrency in another comment, I think, which is not possible on the subscription and would just increase the absurd API key rates by 100x as well.
If you have extremely heavy token usage that’s not amenable to subscription usage - and many tasks aren’t - then local models actually start to make financial sense very quickly.

sigbottle [user?id=sigbottle] 11 hours ago [item?id=50639118] | root [#50637909] | parent [#50638538] | prev [#50639012] | next [#50638656] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I mean, when formal logic was developed in the 20th century, Russel's first philosophy program was trying to bring type theory style intuition to a universal description language and it seemingly failed (both for internal reasons, e.g. Carnap's LP didn't use type theory and was much more extensional, and for external reasons, e.g. logic's place in philosophy as a whole).
When it comes to formalizing messy domains I honestly don't see constrained, defined space manipulation as primary. AI is a perfect example of that - GOFAI failed, LLM's succeeded.
I've been saying, Math 2.0, if it ever does work, will need to be able to bridge the semi-messy gap without saying "the world reduces to mathematical formalisms". Because even if that's true, in practice you don't actually behave like that, right? I mean, the Carnap tried and legit tried talking in L-language, so there are some odd figures here.
But we often have this dualism between "the messy ambiguous stuff out there" and "the formal concrete stuff over here". Would be interesting to see AI bridge "simple but hard" philosophical problems like these

gosub100 [user?id=gosub100] 4 hours ago [item?id=50642358] | root [#50637909] | parent [#50639457] | prev [#50639557] | next [#50644818] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Fusion isn't a math problem needing to be solved. The physics are well understood. It's an engineering problem that needs "solving" - if it's even solvable. Just for starters, you need to create an environment hotter than the core of the sun. Then you need to harvest the energy in a way that is net-positive. The best possible case has about 50% loss. So make ~3X the energy you need so you can produce 1.5x units of electricity, take the 1 to power the fusion and sell the remaining .5.
I'm not sure how AI can help solve that problem; I won't say never but it doesn't present itself as an AI problem.

 [vote?id=50640162&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220#50640162]
sigmoid10 [user?id=sigmoid10] 8 hours ago [item?id=50640162] | root [#50637909] | parent [#50639894] | prev [#50640015] | next [#50639625] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

It's not like LLMs can understand math logic and fail to understand business logic. It's that math is fundamentally logical and business... well simply isn't. There is so much emotion, bad faith and limited information you can't formalize it into any rule based system that is guaranteed to give you a correct output. You'd need Lean for business, which I'm not even sure can exist under the given constraints.

reply [reply?id=50640162&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220%2350640162]

 [vote?id=50640431&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220#50640431]
gobdovan [user?id=gobdovan] 8 hours ago [item?id=50640431] | root [#50637909] | parent [#50640162] | next [#50639625] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

There are always ways to formalise seemingly illogical domains and either it's very simple (P&L, simple profit projections) or very complex and require simulation (dynamic systems modeling, game theory, agent-based simulation).
Whenever you believe emotion cannot be modeled properly, you can treat it as noise. If, on the other hand, you believe you can use it to predict future events, then congrats, you've just incorporated it under a logical model, even if that model is probabilistic.

layer8 [user?id=layer8] 7 hours ago [item?id=50640892] | root [#50637909] | parent [#50639625] | next [#50641264] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

AGI includes the ability to reason over things that aren’t a clean formal system, and where you don’t have a Lean verifier to test the correctness of your reasoning. Therefore I’m skeptical of extrapolating pure classical-logic reasoning to AGI, and I’m even skeptical that current LLMs could have solved these math problems without access to a Lean verifier.

soulofmischief [user?id=soulofmischief] 5 hours ago [item?id=50641471] | root [#50637909] | parent [#50640892] | next [#50641264] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

It may not have been able to construct a verifiable proof without Lean, but current frontier models are decent at working with high level maths on a conceptual level before something like Lean would even be relevant.
In my own experience doing maths with them, I find there are bigger issues than verifiability such as keeping the model on track and out of the gutter. These models are great at appearing busy while doing nothing useful.
Levent also brought a good bit of maths to the table, so it might be more interesting to wonder if what Levent contributed was latent in the model, if the model eventually would have discovered this solution on its own in a loop, or if Levent contributed key missing input or otherwise managed to put the model in a state it couldn't have reached on it's own.

> The effectiveness of Claude models in science will certainly play a role in the value of the stock over time
I really doubt that's the case. The company doesn't capture any economic value from those results, other than prestige and PR. And using the model that solved a famous math problem isn't even an edge you have over others, your competitors have access to the exact same model if needed. Companies just route their inference to whatever is the best current model for a given task, there is no stickiness. Which is why you see AI providers trying to develop actual products. Unfortunately by doing so they will compete with their own customers.
We still haven't seen a business model for frontier AI labs that seems to be economically viable

 [vote?id=50643176&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220#50643176]
nater5000 [user?id=nater5000] 2 hours ago [item?id=50643176] | parent [#50637909] | prev [#50639718] | next [#50639436] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

>If even the prestige from Riemann's Hypothesis couldn't boost ANT
This isn't a very valid take away from all of that. Although I do concur that solving the Riemann Hypothesis didn't specifically do much in terms of building any significant hype around Anthropic, that's surely because it was considered inevitable at this point due to the other problems solved by these labs recently. From that broader perspective, I do think both labs (and AI, in general) are receiving a ton of hype (appropriately). It's just "spread out" so it's hard to see.
Beyond that, trying to correlate such hype with the stock price of one of these companies is just foolish. The market is primarily driven by macroeconomics. Anthropic is entering the market just as there's a tech sell-off occurring. Any sort of positive effect from this kind of news will definitely be overshadowed by the movements of the market as a whole. Similarly, solving the Riemann Hypothesis doesn't really change any of the financial dynamics of Anthropic significantly. Like, who wasn't convinced to invest in them before they solved the Riemann Hypothesis who will now be convinced because they can? Those who would have been swayed by such capabilities (and have the capital to make an impact) will have already assessed these capabilities a while ago (i.e., my first point).
>Open weight models are to the point that most business cases are completely solved by them
This isn't how business works. Business, as an abstract concept, can't be solved. If open weight models are good enough to solve some business problem as it stands today, but private models are able to "solve" those problems better, than the businesses that use the private models will have the advantage. That advantage is the actual problem of business.
Obviously this is a function of cost effectiveness (if you need to jack up prices 10x compared to competitors because your AI usage is so costly, while your product is only marginally better, than you likely aren't running a very good business), but AI costs really aren't currently a major factor for firms who depend heavily on technology (at least not to the extent that they're willing to take the cost of less capable models).
>Unfortunately, the average business doesn't care that the AI can solve frontier problems: they just need something to summarize emails or give an executive a summary of the day's headlines.
This is misplacing how these labs make money. Sure, the average business may not be able to fully leverage the most capable models, and sure the average business may not be able to warrant spending that premium on such models. But that's not really who the target audience is. Labs like Anthropic and OpenAI are certainly targeting firms where tech is the foundation of their competitive advantage. Those firms really don't have a choice but to spend the money on the best models available, otherwise they face serious risks of their competition jumping ahead because they are.
>The market is just waiting for a cheap provider that is just as turnkey... Which isn't exactly a high barrier these days.
I suspect a company like Google or Microsoft will be the one to take this specific space. Basically, they're able to capture the consumer space at scale. Google, specifically, has clearly demonstrated this as being their goal, and they're doing a good job with it. But the big money in this space aren't going to settle for a "good enough" provider just because their service is easier to use. They'll spend the money and deal with the complexity for the best models since that's where they stand to make the most money.
>IMO there is space for a unicorn to emerge in the next few years to eat everyone's lunch.
Possibly. Although I think such a unicorn would get eaten up by one of these firms, first, before it every got close to being big enough to compete with them at this point.

locknitpicker [user?id=locknitpicker] 11 hours ago [item?id=50639389] | root [#50637909] | parent [#50638072] | prev [#50638112] | next [#50643990] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

> The rest of your paragraph makes it sound like you think cheap & “good enough” AI is what the market wants.
But reality shows that cheap and good enough is exactly what the market wants.
If you bother to check usage metrics, you'll learn that over half of all AI prompts target the smalles and cheapest models. Not fancy "frontier" models.
Take a look at OpenRouter's LLM ranking metrics[1]. The absolute top model is DeepSeek v4.1 flash. From the top 10, the only non-flash, non-free models are Claude Opus 5.5 and Kimi K3.1 pro. DeepSeek v4.1 flash had over 10 times as many prompts as Opus 5.5. It isn't even close.
[1] https://openrouter.ai/rankings#top-models [https://openrouter.ai/rankings#top-models]

Investment -> Compute -> Miraculous-things -> Investment...

bob1029 [user?id=bob1029] 4 minutes ago [item?id=50645204] | root [#50640069] | parent [#50640319] | next [#50641088] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

O(n^100) is technically polynomial. But with 4096 bits of key material, this would be absolutely ridiculous to attack.
If this math stuff is making you nervous about your current RSA scheme, I'd recommend moving to ECDSA with P-384 or P-521 curves. P-521 gets you close to the nominal classical strength of AES256 which is effectively immune to quantum concerns. It also saves you from the scaling problems with massive RSA keys.

I'd expect that for the next 5 years. A broken RSA would have a whole lot more direct impact than a broken ML-KEM would. Breaking lattices would be very annoying to people doing long term crypto planning. And would require throwing out a whole lot of work.
Breaking RSA would harm security on millions of websites and devices. And would involve a whole lot of grief and direct harm to a lot of people.
One of those is worse than the other.

Isn't that assuming there is a roughly similar amount of actually invested resources to tackle both set of problems?
Also a little while ago I tinkered to switch the OpenSSL backend on my test server to support Kyber for PQC. The math behind all this is not trivial, obvious, but switching from one algo to the other is actually trivial. Sure it'd be a mess for a bit, especially with... as always unmaintained old systems, that's nothing new.
So I'm not saying breaking the most popular crypto algorithm is a nothingburger BUT I would still argue it wouldn't be the end of the World as we know it.
We are already seeing a bunch of security issue with a lot more boring stuff, like unpatched vulnerabilities.

My first intuition was to agree with you, but on second thought: What is the ROI on breaking crypto for a company that wants to appear trustworthy? Surely, the first reaction would be a lot of negative PR.
And there is nuance here. Let's say you go for low hanging fruits, and break SHA1, 3DES or RSA-1024 completely. Well, you just forced a lot of legacy systems that were already deemed insecure to shut down. Not a whole lot of RoI on your side though.
Breaking the SOTA crypto is probably orders of magnitude more challenging.

> And there is nuance here. Let's say you go for low hanging fruits, and break SHA1, 3DES or RSA-1024 completely. Well, you just forced a lot of legacy systems that were already deemed insecure to shut down. Not a whole lot of RoI on your side though.
If the low-hanging fruit is not relevant anymore, then there's no reason to not include the results along other number theory proofs, and get PR for being so good at math. If the low-hanging fruit is still marginally relevant, the implication makes it a good advertisement for defense contracts.
They've managed to, so far, shrug off the intellectual property theft accusations, the psychological damage to users, the societal issues from labor replacement, the inherent risks of developing smart AI, etc. I find it unlikely that breaking cryptography is what gets them. BoppreH [user?id=BoppreH] 8 hours ago [item?id=50640148] | root [#50640069] | parent [#50640116] | next [#50640846] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

That's about the magic numbers in the ECDSA construction. The Dual_EC_DRBG had an honest-to-god public key embedded in it, and generated "random" numbers by basically encrypting data with this key. The only reasonable explanation is that the NSA holds the private part and is therefore capable of reverse-engineering random sequences generated with it. Hence why they paid the RSA company to use it. It was not subtle at all.

It takes a very similar skill set, and the evaluation of cryptanalysis progress is even more structured than Lean proofs, which makes it more amenable to large-scale LLM efforts. I'm surprised you find this controversial.

One possibility is that OpenAI may be holding back their best models to make fundamental breakthroughs, then using those breakthroughs to build stronger models in a compounding loop.  [vote?id=50638281&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220#50638281]
rao-v [user?id=rao-v] 14 hours ago [item?id=50638281] | parent [#50637720] | next [#50637835] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Open math problems are a beautiful source of frontier-model-hard reinforcement learning signal. They are the magic trifecta of known hard, perfectly resistant to contamination risk and precisely verifiable.
They absolutely will want to use them as impeccable signal for model improvement as long as they can. (It's an interesting theorycraft puzzle to wonder if publishing solutions is a bit about poisioning the well for competitors' RL training ... but I don't actually believe that works)
For what it's worth, I've played with trying to develop new classes of scalable frontier RL problems that scale, and it's not easy to come up with them: https://github.com/kinchahoy/inventing-rl-tasks [https://github.com/kinchahoy/inventing-rl-tasks]. Thoughts welcome, and I'm sorry to report I'm planning to blog a bit on the subject over the holidays.

 [vote?id=50638906&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220#50638906]
dzdt [user?id=dzdt] 12 hours ago [item?id=50638906] | root [#50637720] | parent [#50638462] | next [#50639201] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I would expect big crossover effects in lots of domains. A huge part of the challenge in developing complicated math proofs is in keeping a coherent strategy/narrative/approach across a task which is too large to have the full details in active context at the same time. Humans do this by compressing the problem using layers of higher level objects on top of lower level ones and moving up and down the stack to reason at higher or lower levels. The low-level coherence comes from the existence of the higher level reasoning. I would expect an AI which is good at math proofs to have strong latent skills at that kind of level-shifting which will cross-apply broadly to other tasks.

reply [reply?id=50638906&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220%2350638906]

rao-v [user?id=rao-v] 11 hours ago [item?id=50639201] | root [#50637720] | parent [#50638462] | prev [#50638906] | next [#50638674] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

The secret is that RL improvements generalize beyond their domain! An agent that can put together tricky bits of math from multiple fields efficiently to solve a problem is also one that can cleverly put together libraries to design a better more architected app. Indeed, I think the converse has actually been the big surprise of mid 2026. Training agents with RL to write code made them better at math (and a lot more!). Some generic benefits to problem solving ride along with being really good at applying number sieves and picking the right database for a task!

 [vote?id=50638840&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220#50638840]
CoolestBeans [user?id=CoolestBeans] 12 hours ago [item?id=50638840] | root [#50637720] | parent [#50638462] | prev [#50638699] | next [#50638975] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Math proof is one of the few applications where the validation gap can be closed mechanically. The labs keep posting more and more impressive math results because it is one of the few fields in which there is no impediment to progress. Other fields include hacking and reverse engineering which are well established and have negative implications to potential customers so they can't shout it from the roof tops.
The uncomfortable truth is that because LLM powered agents by their nature cannot reliably check their own work, the need for human verification and validation may limit the productivity boost in most fields to a modest 10 to 30%. Not nothing certainly, but not high enough to charge the prices necessary to cover their compute obligations.
In other words, the volume of math proof results is a signal. But it's a signal about what LLMs and AI may not be able to do.

reply [reply?id=50638840&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220%2350638840]

The last time this came up I looked into it and concluded that Opus probably was nerfed. This was leading up to the Fable release in early November.
The earlier conclusion was from votes through our open source battle system on OpenCode (https://github.com/bottomless/agent-duel [https://github.com/bottomless/agent-duel]) so the data is pretty well controlled. (I've seen other claims of nerfing that are a single time series, but tasks could change.)
I just re-ran the same analysis and there's no nerfing at all right now. In fact Opus 5.5 thinking compared to opponents actually rose slightly in last five days compared to before that.
(Previous analysis is here, I think they also tweaked the model then becuase em-dashes plummeted https://micjm.com/blog/get-ready-for-fewer-opus-5.5-em-dashe... [https://micjm.com/blog/get-ready-for-fewer-opus-5.5-em-dashes/])

The optimal matrix multiplication algorithms are generally not used anyway due to the constants in front. I think maybe Strassens might be used but they also typically have numerical stability problems. I think for the most part the n^3 solution is what’s used in data centers since it’s easy to parallelize to be faster.
I don’t know what the closed weight models are like but the n required for the open weight ones aren’t typically that large (something like n=10k or so).

seizethecheese [user?id=seizethecheese] 13 hours ago [item?id=50638416] | prev [#50637539] | next [#50637581] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

These math proofs are so insanely over my head, but I really want to understand them. I tried Jane Street's socratic tutor skill (https://github.com/janestreet/agent_skills/blob/master/socra... [https://github.com/janestreet/agent_skills/blob/master/socratic-tutor/SKILL.md]) against this pdf and here's what I got:
- Matrix multiplication is multiplying pairs of numbers together then summing them (you probably know this already)
- Without any optimization, this process is o(n^3)
- You can make this faster if you share a multiplication or sum across multiple places
- By sharing sums / multiplication operations the maximum efficiency is o(n^2.25)
I studied Economics, not CS so I'm a bit hazy on what big o actually means, to be honest.

 [vote?id=50638907&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220#50638907]
triCoder [user?id=triCoder] 12 hours ago [item?id=50638907] | parent [#50638416] | next [#50638586] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I can help explain big O, it's a super weird concept:
Let's say you want to know the speed of an algorithm. That's a hard thing to do. First, there's the problem that it's different speeds on different computers. Then there's the problem that speed will be relative to the size of the thing you are processing. Then, there's the issue that what you really want is easy ways to compare algorithms, so you don't really want the exact speed of an algorithm but a quick estimate. Finally, you want to make it mathematically provable.
Big O is so weird because of the last two. If you want a quick estimate, you probably want it to be handwavy. But if you want it mathematically provable, then you don't. It feels like these two should be impossible to fit together. But Big O makes it work by putting algorithms into different buckets. If two algorithms are in the same bucket, you handwave minor differences in speed. But which bucket that it's in is mathematically provable.
The way Big O works is that you build an equation of how long it takes given some input with size n, then you take the largest term and you ignore any constant multipliers and smaller terms, with precise mathematical terminology that lets you do so. So the run time of some algorithm is 3 * n^2 + n, then it would be O(n^2), as that is the largest term.
The perfect place to see this in action is in sorting algorithms. They tend to fall into three main buckets - O(n^2) for slow algorithms that are easy to create, O(n lg n) for fast algorithms that are optimal, and O(n) for super fast algorithms that only work if the data you are sorting has particular properties. So simply by knowing which of these three buckets your sorting algorithm is in, I can tell if you are using a bad algorithm, using a good one, or making assumptions about your data that may or may not be true.

reply [reply?id=50638907&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220%2350638907]

verandaguy [user?id=verandaguy] 13 hours ago [item?id=50638586] | parent [#50638416] | prev [#50638907] | next [#50638600] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

On your last point: when talking about algorithms in the abstract, we still use similar structures to those used in programming; loops and branches (if/else) are the most critical in many of the common complexity analysis cases (big-O, little-O, big-Theta).
Big-O specifically refers to the worst-case time or space complexity of an algorithm. Assume that your algorithm operates on an input of n elements. Then:
- A straight iteration over every element (`for item in items: print(item)`) runs in linear time: O(n) for n elements.
- If your algorithm has a nested loop, and each of the loops has a bound which is either literally n, or derived from n, this is quadratic time (O(n^2)). For example, this naïve search for duplicates:

 for item in items:
 for dupe_candidate in items:
 if item == dupe_candidate:
 print(f"{item} is a dupe!")

This extends into higher powers (O(n^3), O(n^4), etc)) with deeper nesting of loops, and if inner loops are dependent upon other input parameters, you can use a separate variable for that (O(n^m) for example).
- There are also cases where algorithms run in sub-linear time. Two very common cases for that are logarithmic time (O(log(m, n)), which often comes up in algorithms over height-balanced search trees of arity m, and constant time (O(1)), which means that regardless of the input size, the algorithm will run in the same amount of time (which sounds great, until you realize that for large enough values of 1 this is actually really slow -- so you often see these constant-time algorithms find niche applications at large scales where even a very large 1 is still small).
From a math perspective, this is really just modelling a function's behaviour over a given input range, as well as when it tends towards infinity. Understanding your algorithm's time and space complexity as well as the input domain are a very common first step towards identifying the data structures and algorithms which will make up your application.

reply [reply?id=50638586&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220%2350638586]

amomchilov [user?id=amomchilov] 13 hours ago [item?id=50638600] | parent [#50638416] | prev [#50638586] | next [#50641310] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

“Big o notation” are the search terms you’re looking for.
It’s a notation for quantifying the complexity of algorithms, by how the number of operations needed scales with the size of the input (often “n”). For example, greeting every person when you enter a room is O(n), because the number of greetings increases linearly with the number of people in the room. But having every person in a room greet every other person in a room is O(n^2), as the number of greetings scales quadratically with the number of people.

 [vote?id=50643177&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220#50643177]
zahlman [user?id=zahlman] 2 hours ago [item?id=50643177] | parent [#50638416] | prev [#50641310] | next [#50637581] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

All of the big-O notation (and variants) is a way to measure how the demands of a task grow as the input size grows. We care about this because if one program simply does a task twice as fast as another, or one has a bit less startup time than the other, then we could always make up that difference with a faster computer. That is, if the two algorithms are in the same "complexity class" then the problem size doesn't matter and the performance just depends on our hardware. But if one of them uses an algorithm with a lower complexity, then we can — in principle — always make that program come out ahead, regardless of the hardware, by simply making the problem bigger.
Of course, that's at a hand-wavy, theoretical level. We assume that a bunch of smaller pieces simply take a set amount of time (for example, in this matrix multiplication result, we don't care about how long it takes to multiply really big numbers together compared to small ones, mainly because a really big matrix doesn't necessarily have bigger numbers in it than a small one). And we don't care, when we do these proofs, about whether the algorithms are practical. (In fact it's common in the real world that we use a "slow" algorithm because the "faster" one requires impossibly large problem sizes to show its benefit; maybe we'd have to wait past the end of the heat death of the Universe even in the best case). And most importantly, we have to be able to measure the size of the problem in a consistent way. (For matrix multiplication, we choose n as either dimension of a square matrix. This is arguably non-standard, since it means we have n^2 elements of input data!)
We use this notation to mean that we're throwing away constant factors. For example, "show the first million bytes of a file" is O(1): no matter how long the file gets, we just ignore everything after the first million bytes, so it takes the same amount of time for those longer files. And similarly it doesn't matter how much work is involved in "showing" a byte, nor my choice of "million" for the problem description (since it isn't coupled to n). But "show the first half of a file" is O(n): the longer the file gets, the more we have to show, and the amount we have to show is proportional to that input size.
Anyway, with our standard definitions, the obvious way to multiply matrices needs on the order of n^3 operations. That means that the number of operations we have to do, grows as the cube of the matrix side length. This is the textbook algorithm you would have learned in high school, and it's pretty straightforward to find that o(n^3) result: there are exactly n^2 values in the result, and for each of them, we take a column and a row, match up and multiply each pair (n of them), and add those results (n-1 additions to do; of course the bigger n gets, the less the 1 matters).
On the other hand, we could set an a priori theoretical minimum complexity of n^2, because there's that much output data: we have to compute each value in the result somehow, and computing a value involves stating it (which takes at least a specific constant amount of time no matter what).
Progress on this problem has generally involved finding better algorithms that reduce that exponent from 3, towards 2 (i.e. through non-integer values). In early October, OpenAI showed (to my understanding) that there must be a way to get it arbitrarily close to 2.25, then other mathematicians figured out the way. Now OpenAI has (apparently) shown that we can reach 2.25 exactly, and also that we cannot do any better than that.

That's a bug not a feature.
Like when you ask a coding agent to write something and, as part of the solution, it cooks up an entirely new JSON deserialization library or Paxos implementation from scratch as part of the task instead of using (and perhaps improving) a battle-tested library.
Yes, technically the agent succeeded at the assigned programming task. Just like technically this agent succeeded at proving this theorem. But unfortunately the work-product is unmaintainable slop.
Nobody hesitates to say "yes it's correct but it's slop" about AI code. It's time to apply the same rule to AI proofs.

It matters differently for business code that fundamentally solves old problems versus code (or proofs) that solve new problems. What makes the slopborne NIH-syndrome frameworks suboptimal is that there exist solutions to those exact problems already. That does not necessarily apply to frameworks built in the course of a new problem.
In fact, when humans build new mathematical frameworks, that's often touted as one of the important side effects of solving open problems. That could well be just as true here.

 [vote?id=50640465&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220#50640465]
jll29 [user?id=jll29] 8 hours ago [item?id=50640465] | prev [#50637581] | next [#50638049] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Finding lower bounds for fast matrix multiplication also has implication on complexity bounds for natural language parsing algorithms, since the two tasks have been shown to be equivalent, i.e. syntax parsing can be described as a sequence of matrix multiplications [1,2].
[1] Valiant, Leslie G. (1975) "General Context-Free Recognition in Less Than Cubic Time" Journal of Computer and System Sciences, 10(2), 308–315.
[2] Lee, Lillian (2002) "Fast Context-Free Grammar Parsing Requires Fast Boolean Matrix Multiplication" Journal of the ACM, 49(1), 1–15.

black_knight [user?id=black_knight] 5 hours ago [item?id=50641432] | parent [#50638263] | next [#50640647] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

(I wrote this comment for a different thread, but it fits maybe even better here.)
I see how people think this would be the downfall of human mathematics. But I don’t think it is.
Mathematics is not a spectator sport. The goal is (human) understanding. Maybe because school portraits it as a competition to solve exercises, people think otherwise. But the exercises are only a means to an end. We do mathematics to understand abstract concepts. Our method is posing theorems which are meaningful to us and finding proofs of them.
Thus, it does not matter how the proofs come about, as long as they contribute to human understanding. The project of maths continues.
Mathematics isn’t solved, it never will be. We know that LLMs are not oracles (Gödel says hello), thus there will be problems even they can’t solve. But we will invent new theories, new problems to solve (using our brains and LLMs) for many years to come.

reply [reply?id=50641432&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220%2350641432]

 [vote?id=50640647&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220#50640647]
DuncanCoffee [user?id=DuncanCoffee] 7 hours ago [item?id=50640647] | parent [#50638263] | prev [#50641432] | next [#50638750] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

 is math going to become so ridiculously complex and dense than no human can contribute anything meaningful to it?

My 2.25 cents:
- math can be considered discovered, not invented, It's already "out there"
- the problems we want to solve now are the difficult ones, since the easy ones have already been solved
- is there a difference between an unsolved problem, and a demonstration so complex we cannot understand? Unless the solution can be verified experimentally and then used, can we really say we proved it? Or did we just move from one question to another?

reply [reply?id=50640647&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220%2350640647]

 [vote?id=50640817&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220#50640817]
williamdclt [user?id=williamdclt] 7 hours ago [item?id=50640817] | root [#50638263] | parent [#50640647] | next [#50638750] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

> Unless the solution can be verified experimentally
Depends what you mean by "experimentally" but I'd say we can, that's what Lean does. It goes through the proof step-by-step to verify it.
> is there a difference between an unsolved problem, and a demonstration so complex we cannot understand?
Big difference yeah: even if we don't understand the demonstration, if we trust it (which we do thanks to Lean) we can 1. use the result to build other things (more maths, or physical things) and 2. stop spending effort trying to prove/disprove the theorem or trying do impossible things (eg trying to implement a faster matmul than O(n^2.25))

reply [reply?id=50640817&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220%2350640817]

vkgcr [user?id=vkgcr] 9 hours ago [item?id=50640134] | parent [#50638263] | prev [#50638581] | next [#50640495] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Two ideas :
- So far, all of these results are proofs of pre-existing problems / statements, so perhaps LLMs are incapable (lack of world models) of choosing what interesting statements to prove ; the mathematician's role will therefore be to "intuit" what mathematical statements are interesting and then use LLMs to prove them.
- If the above is false, then we can expect lots of new statements being proven ; the mathematician's role will then be similar to a scientist's, with the difference being that instead of trying to understand and build theories from observations of the physical world, a mathematician will try to understand and build theories from the LLM-generated statements & proofs.

reply [reply?id=50640134&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220%2350640134]

You guess incorrectly. Look up Gödel's incompleteness theorems. In particular they imply that there is no decision procedure for mathematical truth. This applies to LLMs as much as anything.

 [vote?id=50639808&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220#50639808]
jltsiren [user?id=jltsiren] 9 hours ago [item?id=50639808] | parent [#50638263] | prev [#50638361] | next [#50638763] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Pure mathematics may die as a research field, but applied mathematics will live on.
There is always more mathematics to discover. But if there are no people who can truly appreciate it, it's probably not worth doing without concrete applications in mind.
Research in pure mathematics is mostly done, because mathematicians find it interesting. It has never been something people outside mathematics really care about. Having a few mathematicians in each university is probably worth it, because it makes the university a nicer place. Much like having a few people working on other obscure topics. But nobody wants to spend any real money on things like that. Which is why OpenAI and Anthropic are probably outspending the rest of the world in pure mathematics research.

 [vote?id=50641914&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220#50641914]
odyssey7 [user?id=odyssey7] 4 hours ago [item?id=50641914] | root [#50638263] | parent [#50639808] | next [#50638763] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Folks in the cryptography business pay mathematicians to do research.
But pure math otherwise has been a pyramid scheme for a while. You take pure math classes in hopes of one day being able to teach other people pure math. Eventually, after blood and tears, most people are “weeded out” of a system that couldn’t actually provide them a job, no matter how talented they could have been, and almost like a roulette wheel a few of them get to perpetuate the cycle.
Who knows. Maybe with AI liberating everyone from the need to make a living, we’ll actually have more pure mathematicians than before.

gpt5 [user?id=gpt5] 10 hours ago [item?id=50639744] | root [#50638263] | parent [#50638652] | next [#50640264] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Because math is useful even if you don't understand it.
For example, if you can create a provably secure public key based encryption (or alternatively, break any public key based encryption), it's useful no matter how dense and complicated it is, to the point where no human can understand how it works.

tczMUFlmoNk [user?id=tczMUFlmoNk] 13 hours ago [item?id=50638518] | parent [#50637425] | next [#50637434] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

While "Θ(n^2.25)" is better, imho it's still misleading. Matrix multiplication is a problem that can be solved by algorithms whose running times (under various conditions) are functions. "Being Θ(n^2.25)" is a property of a function, not an algorithm, and not a problem. There are matrix multiplication algorithms commonly used whose worst-case running times are most certainly not Θ(n^2.25) (namely, all of them known before last month). One could precisely say that "an optimal algorithm for matrix multiplication has worst-case running time Θ(n^2.25)" or "optimal matrix multiplication takes time Θ(n^2.25)", though personally I think that the paper's formulation is cleanest: the matrix multiplication constant is ω = 2.25.
This may seem pedantic, but misconceptions about the meanings of big-O/Theta/Omega notation are very common (e.g., conflating them with "best/average/worst case" or other such nonsense) and they muddy the waters.

reply [reply?id=50638518&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220%2350638518]

 [vote?id=50639234&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220#50639234]
hansvm [user?id=hansvm] 11 hours ago [item?id=50639234] | root [#50637425] | parent [#50638518] | next [#50641304] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Is literally anyone confused by the notation? Constrained to this problem: There exists an algorithm achieving Θ(n^2.25) runtime. There does not exist an algorithm achieving o(n^2.25) runtime. If we want to be pedantic about function classes vs other extensions of Big-O notation then we can talk about the runtime of some specific class of algorithms associated with this problem and manufacture the function everyone is implicitly referring to. It's a perfectly fine shorthand phrasing, especially given that the domains are different for that overlap in vocabulary.
The best/average/worst case stuff is similarly quite easy. For best, take the lowest runtime for the algorithm across all inputs of length n. Do Big-O on that sequence. Worst-case is the same technique applied to the highest runtime. Average is a bit messy since it pulls in an undefined measure which may or may not related to your application, but once you have a measure it's no harder than the other two to analyze. These are rudimentary functions satisfying the pedantry you're asking for and also exactly corresponding to the normal engineering mentality when they reach for that verbal shorthand. Why do they need further elaboration?

reply [reply?id=50639234&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220%2350639234]

 [vote?id=50638890&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220#50638890]
tczMUFlmoNk [user?id=tczMUFlmoNk] 12 hours ago [item?id=50638890] | root [#50637425] | parent [#50638843] | next [#50639578] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

"Problem" is absolutely not a shortcut for "the optimal algorithm(s)". These are important and distinct concepts in computer science.
Perhaps most obviously, one can define problems that have no correct algorithms, like the halting problem. The study and analysis of these problems is critical, and furthermore undecidable problems are themselves different (there is a hierarchy of undecidability), so it is not even the case that "all problems with no correct algorithms are equivalent".

reply [reply?id=50638890&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220%2350638890]

 [vote?id=50639196&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220#50639196]
emil-lp [user?id=emil-lp] 11 hours ago [item?id=50639196] | root [#50637425] | parent [#50638890] | next [#50639578] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

This is super-pedantic and not at all how we communicate bounds in the field of algorithms research.
We say all the time that a problem has a certain running time, that's not a problem at all, because after all, our main question is what time it takes to solve the problem.
You can simply go to arXiv cs.ds and see for yourself.

Yeah, I think a more correct statement would be "Problem is O/theta(f(x))" means that the "problem is in the set of problems solvable in O/theta(f(x)) operations you care about in the implied computing model".

titzer [user?id=titzer] 2 hours ago [item?id=50643263] | root [#50637425] | parent [#50638518] | prev [#50639578] | next [#50640231] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

> a property of a function, not an algorithm
You wanted to be pedantic and yet this is also a misconception. All computable functions have (some) algorithm to compute them. All algorithms are computable functions (maybe partial). If we're not talking computable functions, I don't know what we are talking about. Also, you got that backwards; functions are mappings from inputs to outputs and algorithms are deterministic processes to compute such outputs; algorithms have complexity bounds and not necessarily functions.

csheehan10 [user?id=csheehan10] 15 hours ago [item?id=50637723] | parent [#50637469] | next [#50637538] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

This paper only shows the lower bound, i.e. that no algorithm can do better than n^2.25.
The upper bound, that n^2.25 can be achieved was shown here: https://github.com/openai/math/blob/main/preprints/Matrix-Mu... [https://github.com/openai/math/blob/main/preprints/Matrix-Multiplication-Nine-Fourths-October-2-2026/paper.pdf]
It was translated into an algorithm here:
https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2026/281/ [https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2026/281/]

Based on the abstract, it looks like no:
> The proof shows that a smaller exponent would compress tensor networks enough to violate a lower
bound for testing whether the entries in each of several independent lists are distinct.
In other words, it sounds like if the exponent were lower, than you could use that to check if two lists were the same without looking at all the entries (or something like that), which is logically absurd.

 [vote?id=50638172&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220#50638172]
scythe [user?id=scythe] 14 hours ago [item?id=50638172] | root [#50637469] | parent [#50637715] | next [#50644098] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Consider that matrix determinants are zero when the rows are linearly dependent. In fact the most efficient way to test if n rows of length n are linearly independent is to invert the matrix using variants of the famous multiplication algorithms. For example, Strassen's famous multiplication optimization also generates an inversion algorithm [1]. So it's not entirely surprising that comparing lists shows up in an evaluation of matrix algorithms.
1: https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19900033776 [https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19900033776]

Pedantry: In order for O(n) or Theta(n) to be relevant, your problem statement needs to have an “n” in it.
So: Multiplying a (1,n) matrix by an (n,1) matrix is linear in n.

dsign [user?id=dsign] 8 hours ago [item?id=50640418] | prev [#50644528] | next [#50638304] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

This is good.
A lot of people have been questioning "what's the point of this kind of math marketing? Does it affect the stock valuation"? Then they'll go and say something negative about these companies, which is often merited and well-founded, but it is, in this particular case, a bit of a cynicism overload.
The world has many problems; and a lot of them require solutions from the humanities. But some of the biggest ones should be taken away from the humanities and given to the scientists and the technologists, and LLMs have a lot of potential to help. Prime among those is the problem of ailment and disease: we have a real chance of shifting from "spiritual acceptance" to "cure", specially considering that whenever a molecular biologist says "it's very complex and we understand but a fraction of it" they are, to a great extent, expressing a combined limit on computation, experimental throughput and human mind architecture, limitations and availability.
Environmental problems are another example. Can LLMs help engineer better, greener energy systems? I'll admit that a matrix multiplication boundary is not the same kind of problem as building a methane or isopropyl energy cell. But I for one would give it a try at having an LLM crack one of those before wondering what the point is.

 [vote?id=50641695&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220#50641695]
zahlman [user?id=zahlman] 5 hours ago [item?id=50641695] | root [#50638304] | parent [#50638732] | next [#50638922] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

> failed to convince me that this tech will ever escape the containment of a terminal / deterministic processes that can be verified.
The problem is the agency we extend to them. We've already shown a willingness on an individual level to basically let them operate our computers at a level that more or less represents owning the computer, while paying for the privilege. And the recent cyberattacks have highlighted how much stuff is needlessly connected to the Internet in a way that can result in real damages if maliciously operated. And the recent Huggingface stuff, explosion of CVEs etc. has shown that Internet-connected systems may be vulnerable to a frankly embarrassing extent in spite of whatever security theatre we have.
Put those pieces together and the potential for catastrophe is pretty clear. The terminal and deterministic processes are not at all meaningful "containment"; if anything they're the opposite. "Containment" would be if agent harnesses could somehow only possibly exist on physically airgapped hardware.
And yes, of course these risks — like, the knobs to turn to cause problems — existed pre-LLM. But now there is ridiculously more potential to automate attacks in fuzzy ways that couldn't directly be scripted, and ridiculously more compute that people could harness for them.

 [vote?id=50641668&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220#50641668]
turzmo [user?id=turzmo] 5 hours ago [item?id=50641668] | parent [#50638558] | next [#50638773] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

One of the issues with translating results like these to the real world is this: we know that the cost (in time) for multiplying matrices is C N^2.25, where C is some constant. Theoretical computer scientists are interested in driving down that exponent as low as possible, independent of what C is.
In this case, and in many others, optimal algorithms have extremely large C's. In fact so large that there is no practical reason to ever use them. My understanding is that all matrices are multiplied in practice naively (O(n^3)) or with Strassen's algorithm (O(n^2.8something)) but that none of the newer (20th century and beyond) algorithms are practical.

reply [reply?id=50641668&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220%2350641668]

 [vote?id=50638773&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220#50638773]
traes [user?id=traes] 12 hours ago [item?id=50638773] | parent [#50638558] | prev [#50641668] | next [#50637781] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Nope. In fact, this is the exact opposite kind of result: no matter what advances we make, matrix multiplication will never get faster than the best currently known algorithm (in the usual asymptotic sense.)

Well, if you’re going to make this argument, you should probably pick something other than Riemann, which everyone assumed was true anyway. Similarly, tightening the complexity bounds on matrix math is cool, I guess, but the only practical impact is that it rules out some miracle that would make LLMs less expensive to run.
Aside: I’m pretty sympathetic to people who say that an AI-slop proof that humans cannot understand is useless, when the underlying result is essentially of zero consequence. The only point of the exercise is to generate the mathematical leap necessary to prove the thing.

Because people see all this hype, all this astronomical spending, and yet they don't see their lives improving.
For a couple years now they see constant claims like "100x productivity" and "what used to takes multiple years now takes a quarter" but they haven't seen the technology they use improve, not in any real way for the user at least. Hell personally I still encounter bugs regularly on the facebook and instagram apps.
For a lot of people "coding is solved" probably doesn't seem like a good thing at all actually. Why should they be excited about the tech industry being able to generate magnitudes more code at apparently higher quality when big tech's actions are so widely hated. facebook and google are the very face of surveillance capitalism. A technology that makes the companies that indiscriminately and invasively slurps up all their data even better at doing that? The companies making records profits despite constantly being in the news about the fines they have to pay for breaking the law. And now they hear about things like Flock and Palantir and the dystopian future they promise. People like Musk, Zuck, Bezos, Thiel are incredibly unpopular and now these tech billionaires announce that they built AI that is going to be everywhere and do everything for them. Why should they be excited about that?
People also very recently spent a number of years being told they needed to adopt blockchain and nfts, that they needed to get on the metaverse before it was too late. And now the same crowd who were trying to sell them that are saying they need to use AI to not end up in the permanent underclass.
I just don't think there's much optimism out there right now.

Under these assumptions, this advice can make sense; in any other case, it doesn't.

TLDR gives a lower bound to an algorithm, and a previous OpenAI result gave the upper bound so we know such an algorithm should exist. We still don’t know the actual algorithm, nor if it is galactic or practical (my bet it’s galactic), so not practical yet.

reply [reply?id=50638335&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220%2350638335]

 [vote?id=50642202&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220#50642202]
odyssey7 [user?id=odyssey7] 4 hours ago [item?id=50642202] | parent [#50638335] | next [#50638385] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

The big, cool result for human mathematicians to date on this problem has been to make the exponent smaller — doing whatever it took to do so.
With the exponent now bounded — the previously cool result territory has been fully mined out — we may see a pivot to making the algorithms increasingly more practical.
However, one of the most valuable companies in the world builds GPUs, so, having accepted those as a fact of life, and with the big theoretical challenge complete in the terms in which it had been posed for decades, maybe matrix multiplication algorithm improvements won’t have the same pull.

reply [reply?id=50642202&goto=item%3Fid%3D50637220%2350642202]

the paper is much bigger than this. The previous things were upper bounds with the assumption (hope) that the actual lower bound was 2 and that quadratic matmul was possible. This is the exact lower bound (and, sadly > 2).

Given the constants for the known algorithms this is of little practical difference. And even if we find a practical n^2.25 algorithm one day, you would still need pretty large matrices for it to make any difference (i. e. billion rows matrices are only ~200x slower with n^2.25 than with quadratic).
